# Chapter 3 Smart Air Pollution Monitoring and Prediction System Using Deep Learning Enabled IoT Technology

K. M. KARTHICK RAGHUNATH^([²](#fn2))

## Abstract

The series of issues with the engines of modern-day automobiles is growing at an alarming rate. Problems emerge when emissions exceed acceptable limits from the cluster of vehicles in a particular region. Despite the fact that fuel usage has changed significantly, growing metropolises and industrialized scenarios are still considered significant factors that cause air pollutants. DL advances have made it feasible to create prediction systems relying on facts (real-time data) for pollutant evaluation and control. In this chapter, vehicle emissions are being tracked through the internet of things (IoT) technology. The real-time data is used to create a prediction model that forecasts carbon monoxide (CO) levels in a particular region of interest (RoI). Thus, it aids in diversion strategies of traffic flow from polluted to normal regions. For this, vehicles passing through the RoI are equipped with sensors that monitor pollution levels. Traffic regulators are informed of the prevailing pollutant condition in the concerned RoI, and the impact of the vehicles are monitored by utilizing ensemble learning (EL) methods. EL utilizes several base classifiers to refine the accuracy level in prediction. When predicting the pollution load at a particular RoI, an EL model uses historical data together with the most recent data from sensors to make its predictions. Thus, a smart emission monitoring system may be used to keep an eye on the pollution level while keeping it under control and support managing the traffic flow and pollution load at the concerned RoI.

## 3.1 Introduction

Air pollution is a big issue in modern civilization, especially as the number of people moving to cities grows. Consequently, considerable efforts are focused on developing viable monitoring systems, which include sensors and cameras that collect data in real-time \[[1](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_1)\]. Air pollution has been found to have a significant impact on human health in industrially developing or developed nations when there is no infrastructure in place to monitor or regulate it. According to recent studies, there is a strong link between air pollution and ailments such as asthma and lung disease. The World Health Organization (WHO) has established rules to limit the levels of specific gases such as nitrogen dioxide (NO[2]), sulfur dioxide (SO[2]), and ozone (O[3]) in the atmosphere. Air quality monitoring (AQM) stations, which are very exact and accurate, are used to calculate the air quality index (AQI) and monitor pollution. They are extremely accurate and reliable in monitoring a wide spectrum of air contaminants.

However, these stations still trail in three areas: (i) infrastructure, which is required for installation because of the large size; (ii) operational needs are rather sophisticated; and (iii) ongoing costs of setup, daily maintenance, and calibration. Due to these faults, the number of installations is reduced, resulting in discrete AQM Networks with little data on air pollution. The use of wireless network technologies in monitoring diverse real-time sensor data, such as temperature, pressure, and air pollution, has been allowed by recent improvements in embedded electronics. The devices’ data is wirelessly transferred to a database on a distant server for further analysis and processing. Because of wireless data transmission, the notion of using small, inexpensive AQM motes has surely grown in popularity. The purpose of this study is to discover a good forecast and predict a prototype or model for the decrease or increase of certain air pollutants such as CO, O[3], SO[2], and NO[2], all of which are deemed detrimental by WHO recommendations. For time-series forecasting, three machine learning (ML) methods were utilized and tested: Linear Regression, Random Forest, XGBoost, and ARIMA. All of these models have a wide variety of applications and have high predictive ability as well as generalization power.

By the year 2050, a minimum of 70% of the world’s population will live in cities, necessitating efficient systems for monitoring and forecasting air pollution. The least contaminated regions are North America and Australia, followed by Central Europe, while the most polluted are India and Asia. Even though there isn’t a lot of data for Africa and South America, data that is available suggests that pollution levels of air are also high in that region. According to the WHO research, every year, 6,00,000 children under the age of 15 die as a result of air pollution. According to studies, air pollution kills 6.9 to 7.1 million people each year, and 91% of the population of the globe breaths toxic air. Children under the age of five in underdeveloped countries breathe filthy air at a rate of 98%. The global economy lost $5 trillion in welfare due to premature mortality caused by air pollution.

The internet of things (IoT) paradigm has emerged in the last few years, digital communication transceivers, microcontrollers, and an appropriate protocol stack that lets them to connect with one another and with users, allowing them to become a vital component of the Internet. Devices like household appliances, surveillance cameras, sensors, and cars may now create massive volumes of data that can be evaluated and used to develop new applications thanks to this feature. Recent advancements in the field of IoT technologies have enabled people to create effective methods that consist of many sensors connected wirelessly to monitor various air contaminants in different locations, either through Wi-Fi or Bluetooth for adjacent mobile networks or regions, and satellites for remote or isolated regions. To assess developing trends and enable near real-time monitoring, these amounts of different data may require parallelization employing big data and cloud computing technologies \[[2](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_2)\]. Monitoring systems enable us to obtain highly qualified data that may subsequently be utilized to gain a better understanding of pollution.

It is expected that air pollution forecasting systems would use the AQI, which describes each pollutant’s value, such as particle matter (PM) or carbon dioxide concentration (i.e., CO[2], PM10, PM2.5, etc.), to identify sites with a lot of pollution. Governments may use predictive air pollution technology to help them develop more intelligent solutions and preventative measures to address air quality challenges. Although the literature contains a variety of models and methodologies for forecasting air contamination, they are in general be classified by two main groups. Firstly, models or prototypes for tracking pollution production, dispersion of pollution, and transmission. These models’ prediction outcomes are generated by numerical simulations. The second category includes applying models of ML algorithms, statistical learning algorithms, and deep learning algorithms. These software models try to learn through the distribution of the data and patterns which are directly extracted from the input data.

Many authors have been encouraged to create and deploy alternative prediction models as a result of recent developments in deep learning methods and the large quantity of the data accessible, as mentioned in more depth in the related work section. Despite this, the findings in the literature remain inconclusive \[[3](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_3)\]. As a result, air pollution forecasting remains a vital and active study field. The use of photographs for air pollution prediction is, in particular, an unknown topic that requires greater study.

Because of their influence on citizens’ daily lives, high-quality projections on this subject are crucial. Knowing ahead of time of high-pollution occurrences can lead to regulations like traffic restriction, the closing of public places like schools, and suggestions to reduce exposure for sensitive populations. As a result, high-quality predictions need adequate detection approaches that are suited to the task at hand and can solve intrinsic problems such as handling class imbalance and utilizing multimodal data.

Assessment of air quality is the most significant part of the air pollution monitoring and control. The suitability of air supply for a specific application is determined by its qualities. Criteria air pollutants are a group of contaminants that are extensively spread across the United States. These pollutants might be the reason to harm the people living area, environment, people’s health, and their property. The following pollutants have been designated as criteria pollutants:

*   Ozone (O[3]);
*   Sulfur dioxide (SO[2]);
*   Nitrogen dioxide (NO[2]);
*   Carbon monoxide (CO); • Particulate matter (PM);
*   Lead (Pb).

The EPA, local, tribal air contamination or pollution control agencies, state, and use the air quality system (AQS) to collect data on ambient air pollution from thousands of sensors. AQS also includes descriptive information about each monitoring station (such as operator and its geographical location), weather data, and data quality/assurance control information data from the AQS is used to aid in Attainment or Non-Attainment designations, monitor air quality, do modeling for permit review analysis, analyze State Implementation Plans for the area which is in Non-Attainment, and execute other air quality management duties \[[4](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_4)\]. The Clean Air Act requires that AQS data be utilized to generate reports for Congress.

The standards differ because different contaminants have different impacts. There are guidelines for short-term as well as long-term average durations for some contaminants. Short-term standards were developed to protect against acute or short-term health impacts, whereas long-term standards were developed to protect against long-term health problems ([Table 3.1](#ch3.xhtml#tab3_1)).

[Table 3.1 Potential Health Consequences of the Air Quality Index (AQI)](#ch3.xhtml#R_tab3_1) \[[4](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_4)\]

  
| AQI Value || Range || Color || Recommendatory |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0–50 || Good || Green || Simply least bother or nothing will be hazardous. |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 51–100 || Moderate || Yellow || Individuals who are unusually sensitive to the outdoors should restrict their time outside. |
| 101–150 || Unhealthier for group of sensitive people || Orange || Excessive outdoor activity should be avoided by active adults, children, and people suffering from respiratory illnesses like asthma. |
| 151–200 || Harmful to health || Red || The prolonged outdoor activity must be avoided by active adults, children, and people suffering from respiratory illnesses like asthma; everyone else must limit their time spent outside for long periods of time. |
| 201–300 || Dangerous and unhealthy condition || Purple || The outdoor activity must be avoided by active adults, children, and people suffering from respiratory illnesses like asthma; everyone else has to limit their time outside for lengthy periods of time. |
| 301–500 || Very hazardous to human health || Maroon || Outside physical activity must be avoided by everyone. |

Carbon dioxide (CO[2]), nitrogen oxides (NOx), hydrocarbons (HCs) or volatile organic compounds (VOCs), PM, and carbon monoxide (CO) emissions from vehicles have become a major cause of air pollution in many areas. Increased traffic congestion, especially along major roads, has the reason to drastically degrade air quality and increase contaminant emissions. These emissions increase the risk of illness and death for drivers, commuters, and people who live adjacent to highways, according to epidemiological research, estimations of proposed vehicle emission standards (VES), and environmental impact assessments (EIA) for WHO’s specific road projects.

This study explores the quantity of health risks and air pollution to near and on-road inhabitants that might happen as a result of repeated traffic congestion, such as weekdays (Monday to Friday) in rush hour traffic. Recurring congestion can lead to more chronic and frequent exposures, as well as an increase in long-term health risks \[[5](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_5)\]. Despite the fact that such occurrences may have a considerable impact on some acute health outcomes, such as asthma exacerbation, “the occurrence of overcrowding” such as that activated by a disabled vehicle (due to accident), is not addressed. Predictive risk assessment methodologies are used in this study, including emissions, traffic simulation models, pollutant dispersion, and its risk, as well as an incremental study or analysis that analyzes congestion-related and congestion-free outcomes. To investigate the effects of important factors on the predicted additional risk, a brief sensitivity analysis is performed.

The majority of air pollution approaches rely on unprocessed or processed data, such as pollutant sensory measurements from the past. The innovation of our study lies in the use of multi-modal data consisting of sensor measurements and camera pictures, distinguishing our study from earlier approaches in the literature. Combining all these features helps to address issues associated with the prediction job, such as the availability of class unbalanced data, incomplete, and possibly noisy information derived from a single data source, and vulnerability to adversarial assaults. Consequently, the resultant predictive model looks to be more robust and accurate than previously published solutions.

### 3.1.1 Background And Research Motivation

Air pollution commonly occurs when dangerous and unwarranted/unauthorized quantities of specific chemicals such as gases, particles, and biological molecules are introduced into the atmosphere. Excessive emissions have apparent repercussions, such as disease and mortality among populations and other living species, as well as crop damage. Liquid droplets, Hard particles, and smokes are all types of air pollution, and they are classified as follows. The combustion of fossil fuels from vehicles, as well as industrial, domestic, and agricultural sources, are all common contributors to air pollution. The critical concern air pollutants are nitrogen oxide, SO[2], and CO. They are all that are described as ‘primary’ pollutants since they are emitted directly into the atmosphere. Secondary pollutants are created in the atmosphere, primarily as a result of UV exposure to primary pollutants. Ozone and secondary organic aerosol (SOA) are common secondary pollutants. Ozone (O[3]) is a secondary pollutant that forms in the environment whenever NOx and hydrocarbon are exposed to UV light \[[6](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_6)\]. The generation methods of SOA from fossil-fuel burning remain unknown, although it is a major component of Beijing winter haze pollution.

Air pollution causes a wide range of negative health impacts. For six of the most prevalent air contaminants, the EPA has set National Ambient Air Quality Standards (NAAQS). PM, lead, CO, ground-level ozone, NO[2], haze, and SO[2] are the pollutants in question. The occurrence of these pollutants in the circulatory air is owing to a variety of different and widely disseminated sources of emissions. The NAAQS have been established to standardize the safety of public health. In addition, EPA has also established additional NAAQS to standardize the safety of public health which may impact the eye impairment for humans and safety of animal rights. The AQI is used as an indicator to identify the air contamination location-wise and region-wise. CPCB recently introduced the breakpoint concentration method of AQI assessment, which is for individual pollutant AQI estimation followed by the maximum of these as a synergistic AQI level that can be used for decision making. Since the last quarter, the USEPA and CHINA have been using the notion of breakpoint concentration levels in their research and development \[[7](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_7)\]. The additional pollutants that are used to calculate the “good” indicator AQI of daily air quality that might harm people, such as air toxics have been already discussed in [Table 3.1](#ch3.xhtml#tab3_1).

If we can standardize AQI to forecast the concentration and contaminants of air pollutants hourly, the standard for AQI will become more flexible and beneficial to the safety of public health. Consequently, these forecasting systems are of paramount importance to ensure that proper warning has been issued to the public. These forecasting systems can be useful for governance to alert their community about air pollution exceeding the threshold values. They could also be considered for inclusion in the community governance with their existing emission control programs \[[7](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_7)\].

### 3.1.2 Review Methods And Questions

According to Kitchenham’s work \[[8](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_8)\] and the PRISMA guidelines \[[9](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_9)\], we have conducted this review. As a result, the technique for conducting this review is described in the subsections below. Consequently, we drew inspiration for our review’s design by referring the survey of dengue disease surveillance \[[10](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_10)\]. The following are the main research issues addressed in this review:

*   **Q1:** How much has data mining technology and techniques been applied in the epidemiology of air pollution?
    
    To answer this question, we conducted the relevant literature survey with a focus on the epidemiology of air pollution and research works related to data mining tools, technologies, and techniques. We have not set a time-bound limit for this review, but recent work has been given more importance. We have also considered the growing trend of data mining tools, technologies, and techniques during this review.
    
*   **Q2:** Are there any hotspots for research in the studied field?
    
    Our review focused on covering the review survey covering all aspects, regions, countries, and research institutions. We have also focused more on those countries who have more advances pertaining to our review.
    
*   **Q3:** Identify the focus areas of air contamination in which Data mining has been used previously.
    
    To resolve the issue of finding focus areas based on the question Q3, we have filtered through various categories of epidemiological work related to air pollution and its application areas. In the end, data mining tools, technologies, and procedures showed three types of epidemiological investigations of air pollution in the survey.
    
*   **Q4:** Which data mining techniques were used?
    
    To resolve the issue of finding the data mining techniques and algorithms involved, we conducted a vast review and found various data mining techniques pertaining to air pollution prediction and found four major categories which we will discuss subsequently.
    
*   **Q5:** What are the current work’s limitations?
    
    To overcome the limitation and focus on resolving the issues, we classified all the issue techniques pertaining to air pollution prediction under a few categories and strategically tried to address all the major issues that occurred in the existing systems.
    
*   **Q6:** What potentially fruitful directions haven’t been pursued yet? Finally, in Q6, we discussed possible future actions.

This chapter has been organized as follows: [Section 3.1](#ch3.xhtml#sec3_1) provides an overview of Air pollution and health risks due to vehicle traffic. [Section 3.2](#ch3.xhtml#sec3_2) discusses relevant work that has been done to address the challenge of air pollution forecast and prediction. The architecture of the proposed models and the data preprocessing techniques are explained in [Section 3.3](#ch3.xhtml#sec3_3). [Section 3.4](#ch3.xhtml#sec3_4) deals with the ensemble learning (EL) based prediction. In [Section 3.5](#ch3.xhtml#sec3_5), we provide the evaluation results of our models and discuss them. Finally, [Section 3.6](#ch3.xhtml#sec3_6) concludes the chapter.

## 3.2 Literature Survey

Yi et al. \[[11](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_11)\] proposed, a large amount of research was devoted to the prediction of air pollution, taking both standard and deep learning techniques into consideration. Even though many alternative models have been developed, they all rely on pollution data obtained from sensors at certain times and locations to some extent. The gaseous species and particulate matter with the values PM-2.5 and PM-10 are regarded pollutants are typically the following, i.e., CO, SO[2], O[3], and NO[2]. Integrated into the system are meteorological data such as temperature, rainfall, humidity, and wind speed wherein few systems even incorporate weather prediction indicators information. Fan et al. \[[12](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_12)\], discussed the modeling and preprocessing phases are often carried out in distinct steps. An important stage in the data preparation pipeline is feature extraction. The regularly utilized strategies incorporate discriminant examination, factor investigation, group examination and head part investigation (PCA). Matching along with aggregation by location and time, together with extrapolation and interpolation techniques, are used to achieve data fusion.

Qi et al. \[[13](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_13)\] explained the stage of feature analysis or interpolation is performed separately, certain methods include this stage directly into the model design. Several 100 techniques have been published in the literature, and they may be divided into two main groups. The first group of techniques is concerned with forecasting the amount of contaminants present (such as NO[2], PM-2.5, PM-10, etc.). Kalajdjieski et al. \[[14](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_14)\] proposed the toxin and pollutant method arrangement into two fundamental gatherings. In the principal class of strategies, contaminations are anticipated by determining how many toxins and pollutants, for example, NO[2], PM-2.5, PM-10, and so forth. Because of such regulation and air quality proposals, European Union and other public ecological associations have fostered their own principles and rules for these poisons. Air quality is estimated by the AQI, which reflects how perfect or unclean the air is, just as any related wellbeing perils, particularly for in danger populaces. Health effects that may arise after a few days of exposure to polluted air are the subject of this study. In order to arrive at this number, the highest AQI values for each of the previously specified criteria pollutants are used.

Li et al. \[[15](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_15)\] proposed the AQI or air pollution index techniques, which are included in the second group of methods for forecasting the pollution level. The labeling of pollutant data is the most significant distinction between the two kinds of techniques. When it comes to supervised classification algorithms, the first category of methods does not require labeling because the goal is to predict actual pollutant levels. However, the second category of methods does require labeling fine-grained data because it will be used for supervised algorithms.

Corizzo et al. \[[16](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_16)\] proposed autoencoder models succession to-arrangement models neural organizations that incorporate direct indicators as troupes, Bayesian organizations, and multi-name classifiers are a portion of different strategies that have been utilized as of recent years. The AQI will be more responsive and better for the health of the community, if a prediction system based on the percentages of certain pollutants and susceptible of predicting air quality hourly-based is developed; because AQM systems are essential for communities. By letting environmental regulators to select between “on-demand” reductions in emissions, operational planning, and maybe even emergency action, they can play a vital role in health alerts when pollution levels near stipulated thresholds.

Liu et al. \[[17](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_17)\] discussed the attention-based model used in is another fascinating approach. As a result of this method’s attention mechanism, just the wind measurements are used to generate an encoded result and then used that in the dominant framework. Using attention-based methods, we apply them to all of the available meteorological and pollution information. Wen et al. \[[18](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_18)\] discussed the approaches employ a variety of evaluation methods. They all use standard regression evaluation processes such as the relative prediction error (RPE), mean prediction error (MPE), mean absolute error (MAE), mean absolute percentage error (MAPE), and root mean square error (RMSE).

ARIMA is an abbreviation that represents the autoregressive incorporated moving normal model. For time series anticipating, it is one of the most significant and generally utilized models. Due to its factual properties, flexibility to clarify a wide scope of cycles, and extensibility, this model, at first proposed by Box & Pierce \[[19](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_19)\], had gained critical help in established researchers. Statistical techniques like ARIMA have become extremely prevalent for forecasting air pollution levels including air quality in metropolitan areas over time since the advent of public concern about urban area air quality and living quality. The quality of the data and its representativity are critical factors in ensuring the effective construction of forecasting models. The data preparation stage frequently has an effect on a ML algorithm’s generalization capacity. Missing data are often imputations, outlier observations are removed, data transformations (such as normalization and standardization), and feature extraction are all part of the preprocessing of data. To produce an even information dispersion and to limit information inconstancy, the third stage is often as possible utilized related to the past two stages, which are helpful for getting more definite and complete informational indexes. Finally, a new dataset, frequently smaller but more informative, is generated in the fourth phase. The last advance step incorporates the extraction of elements and the choice of the main ones.

According to Siew \[[20](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_20)\], the capability of the proposed model is to estimate the outcomes for such AQI was investigated, with the outcomes uncovering that it was equipped for creating figures that fell beneath the 95% certainty level. Siew \[[21](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_21)\] proposed the level of self-assurance ARIMA’s success in recent years has been particularly impressive. An exponential smoothing model was used to anticipate daily AQI levels based on findings. While classical statistical models have been used in time-series forecasting, ML models have gained interest as a solution that may be used to make precise forecasts in many scientific areas and spheres of endeavors. To predict air quality, in especially, ML algorithms have been widely used.

D’az Robles et al. \[[22](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_22)\] conducted an investigation on the deployment of a hybrid model incorporating ARIMA and ANN and to forecast the air quality in Chile, specifically P-10 data, using ANNs and ARIMA. ANNs were employed to reflect the nonlinearity observed in the ARIMA regression, allowing for a more accurate representation of the dataset’s linearity. The researchers reasoned that the last model has incredible speculation capacity and outflanks both by using ANNs and ARIMA when utilized autonomously. Using datasets from other pollutant concentrations, Castelli et al. \[[23](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_23)\] designed an evolving framework system for estimating ozone concentrations using GPS up to an hour in advance. The strategy produced accurate findings that outperformed those obtained by state-of-the-art ML approaches. This approach has been employed by a number of researchers to anticipate air quality and pollution levels.

SVR has been examined with artificial neural networks (ANNs) in a study conducted by Muller et al. \[[24](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_24)\]. In general, the authors concluded that SVR performance was superior. A time-series forecasting method combining ANN as well as support vector machines (SVMs) to divide the input space into manageable chunks. This hybrid technique delivers great prediction performance while also allowing for efficient learning, as seen by the findings. Anikender & Pramila \[[25](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_25)\] adopted Multiple Linear Regression Techniques and Principal Component Regression and to anticipate the routine AQI value for Delhi, India, based on prior data of AQI and meteorological indicators. The Research work was funded by the Indian government. With the use of historical data from the years 2000–2005 and several calculations, they anticipate the daily AQI for the year 2006. Multiple linear regression was used to compare the 2006 AQI predicted value with the observed value of 2006 AQI for each of the four seasons (summer, post-monsoon, and winter). Principal component analysis (PCA) is a technique used to determine whether the independent variables are related or not. The principle components were used to minimize the number of data set as well as to eliminate covariance among the predictor variables while doing multiple linear regression. In the winter, Principal Component Regression is used to predict the AQI. AQI forecasts were based only on meteorological data, and no attention was given to fine particulate matter that might have a negative impact on human health.

Huixiang et al. \[[26](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_26)\] identified two separate towns, one in Beijing and one in Italy. In accordance with two publicly accessible datasets, have projected the AQI for Beijing and the quantity of NO[2] in Italy. As of August 2018, the Beijing Public Works Environmental Center has released its first dataset, which includes features including continuously aggregated AQI, NO[2], PM-2.5, PM-10, O[3] and SO[2] concentrations in Beijing. Between

December to August 2018, this dataset has 1,738 occurrences. The second Dataset, which contains 9,358 occurrences, was gathered from an Italian city between March 2004 and February 2005 for the purpose of this study. The properties listed below are contained inside this dataset. CO, nonmethane HCs, benzene, NO[2], and NO[2] concentrations on an hourly basis. However, they concentrated their efforts mostly on NO[2] prediction since it is the most important variables for air quality assessment. The approaches Random Forest Regression and Support Vector Regression methods were utilized to predict the AQI and NO[2] concentrations. SVR outperforms RFR in terms of accuracy in forecasting AQI, whereas RFR outperforms SVR in terms of accuracy in predicting NO[2] concentration.

The information on air pollution during the time period of 2009 to 2017 was examined by Nidhi et al. \[[27](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_27)\], who also provided a critical assessment of Delhi, India’s air pollution trend through 2016 to 2017. Their predictions included SO[2], ozone (O[3]) benzene, NO[2], PM-2.5, CO, and a range of other pollutants. Earlier executing data, utilizing Time-series relapse anticipating, they anticipated future levels of the pollutants indicated. The monitoring stations in the area AnandVihar and Shadipur in Delhi are under investigation as a result of the findings of this research. PM-10 concentrations have increased significantly, while NO[2] and PM-2.5 values have also risen, suggesting a rising degree of pollution in Delhi. A drop in CO, a rise in NO[2], an increase in ozone, a decrease in benzene, and a rise in SO[2] are all predicted for this year’s air quality measurements.

Mohamed & Rakesh \[[28](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_28)\] evaluated the ratio of multiple air pollutants (SO, NO[2], CO, PM-10, and NO) by the span of day and week and dissect the impact of climatic factors like wind velocity, temperature, and dampness on the recorded air contaminations utilizing the WEKA Tool. The contamination control leading group of Karnataka gathered the information. The investigation discovered that when the ZeroR calculation is utilized related to the WEKA tool, the focus levels of air toxins increment during normal working days, especially at the hour of pinnacle hours of the day, and drop at the hour of ends of the week and excursions. The study illustrates the association or dependence between environmental variables such as humidity, wind velocity and temperature, and the air pollutants such as SO[2], NO, PM-10, CO, and NO[2] using simple K-means Clustering methods. In time series analysis, delays are used to quantify series autocorrelation which important phenomena that occurs in data sets. For forecasting, the selection of appropriate time delays and the removal of redundant information are crucial. A greater understanding of the underlying process may be gained by using this method in association with other forecasting techniques. The examination study was done to decide how much slack factors for every toxin and molecule through utilizing autocorrelation capacity and partial autocorrelation. First and second lag Partial autocorrelation graphs indicate gains and declines, respectively. With an 80% confidence level, only the first and second legs were judged important for all pollution series, leading to the creation of 10 additional variables. Finally, the final highlighted variables relate to the rolling mean of each pollutant series across a 24-hour time window.

Kazem et al. \[[29](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_29)\] employed the WHO-recommended AirQ software, which offers quantifiable amount of data such as SO[2], O[3], PM-10 and NO[2] on the health of residents in the city of Tehran, Iran. All-cause mortality, cardiovascular illnesses, and respiratory disorders were all considered in terms of health implications. Pollutant PM-10 previously ha d greatest impact on the city’s populace is 8,700,000 and added to an all-out mortality of 2,194 fatalities out from 47,284 numbers in a year, as per the conclusion of the exploration. The total extra mortality cases attributed to Ozone, NO[2], and SO[2] are 819, 1,050, and 1,458, respectively. The findings indicate that the city has a serious air pollution problem and that the city’s health burden from air pollution must be reduced.

Gunasekaran et al. \[[30](#ch3.xhtml#ref3_30)\] conducted a study to analyze the air quality in Salem city of Tamil Nadu state, India, during the time of April 2010 to March 2011. The yearly normal degrees of toxins like sulfur dioxide, nitrogen-oxide, and PM in this area are within the acceptable range of guideline, per the results of this study. However, the yearly average PM-10 value is somewhat higher than the national guideline. Additionally, the monthly average PM-10 concentrations exceeded the standard threshold with the exception of July to October.

## 3.3 System Architecture

The proposed system’s framework is defined via architectural layout, which exhibits the practice of determining the required software, hardware, and associated interfacing components. It establishes the structure and behavior of the system and several other aspects from multiple perspectives. The system that has been designed is small and light enough to fit in any vehicle. As a result, dangerous gaseous concentrations from moving vehicles are mitigated through further prediction processes. It ensures a significant shift in air quality management and control strategies. This entire physical system is divided into two segments modules. The elaborated description of accounted modules are given in subsections.

### 3.3.1 Module 1: Data Perception

In this study, SO[2], O[3], NO[2], CO, and Particulate Matter (PM10) are five specific air pollutants from vehicle emission. Pollutant emissions from vehicles are detected with the use of sensors. The sensors employed for experimental trials include MQ7, MQ131, Mic 2714, SO[2], and PMS5003. It’s well known that MQ7 has a high sensitivity to CO, Mics 2714 detects NO[2], MQ131 senses O[3], and SO[2] sensor is utilized to trace SO[2] emissions, and finally, PMS5003 is utilized to monitor PM10.

The Raspberry PI 4 modal B board collects the sensing data and process it. The Raspberry PI has been configured to gather sensed data from several sensors and correlate it to predetermined standards to evaluate the accuracy. Five LEDs are incorporated to show the present state of various pollutants. The LED indicators turn red if the detected values are higher than the preset ones; otherwise, it shows green indications to exhibit that the readings are within the acceptable range. If the emission levels continue to be higher than the threshold, Raspberry PI sends the information to the connected GSM module for further processing.

The GSM module provides detailed information and notifications to the users and traffic administrators as messages. An illustration of the architecture model is depicted in [Figure 3.1](#ch3.xhtml#fig3_1). The Raspberry PI board is wired up with the sensors. Sensor data is continuously sent into the Raspberry PI device. A circuit connects the microcomputer to those LED indicators. An interface connects the GSM module to the board. The GSM component includes a cellular sim card.

![Figure 3.1 System architecture.](images/fig3_1_B.jpg)

[Figure 3.1 System architecture.](#ch3.xhtml#R_fig3_1)

Each value obtained during a specific period from the Raspberry PI board is saved as a portion of the dataset. This assists us in keeping tabs on how the atmosphere is changing over time. The obtained information is utilized to build and train the models that perform predictions and analyzes on the complete construction of the dataset with sufficient values. The dataset is made up of real-time data gathered from a wide range of sensors in the field. Each model requires the extracted feature set as an input. The model uses a machine-learning algorithm as base predictor and an EL predictor model to refine the prediction accuracy using real-time and past datasets.

### 3.3.2 Module 2: Data Preprocessing

We did research on New Delhi pollutant sites that are among the most polluted cities in the entire globe. Central Control Room for Air Quality Management (CCRAQM) provides the actual data. Data preparation is the first step in analyzing these readings. The learning phase is preceded by a number of preprocessing processes. If a variable is found to be incorrect, it’ll not impact the entire data set. Instead, the parameter will be labeled empty or, if applicable, substituted with results calculated from the CCRAQM, avoiding the removal of the entire row. Imputation is used to retrieve the appropriate values when there are missing ones. The missing data regarding pollutants are imputed based on the measurements from the actual observation sites. For the remaining incorrect or incomplete information, the k-NN technique was employed to impute them using the prior imputation procedure. All the accounted datasets have a missing value/incomplete information of less than 1.3%.

As a result, the factor relevance won’t be polluted by range or unit effects. The input and intended data are then adjusted to remove any bias. The range of values for all of the raw data is set between 0 and 1. Measurements dominated by sources with a larger scale tend to be given more importance. This is because the normalization increases the pace of model learning while simultaneously supporting the k-NN computation results due to the distance metric being used to determine imputation. Predictive approaches use hourly AQI data with the maximum indices of five pollutants: O[3], PM10, CO, SO[2], and NO[2] to select the optimal features to include in the training and testing process. The accuracy of the present air quality evaluation will be significantly reduced if any of these variables are missing. The AQI (ψ) is computed based on Eqn. (1).

(1)  
*ψ*\={{(*r*[*S**O*[2]],*r*[*C**O*],*r*[*N**O*[2]],*r*[*O*[3]],*r*[*P**M*[10]])}} ≠ *φ**φ*, *O**t**h**e**r**w**i**s**e*  

Eqn. (2) transforms pollutant intensity value ‘r’ at the varying time, ‘t’ into pollutant index ‘*I[p]*.’:

(2)  
$$I\_{p} = \\frac{\\left( {I\_{i}^{H} - I\_{i}^{L}} \\right)}{\\left( {\\delta\_{H} - \\delta\_{L}} \\right)}\\left\\lbrack {\\delta - \\delta\_{L}} \\right\\rbrack + I\_{L}$$  

where; *i* denotes (*O*[3] ∥*PM*10∥ *CO* ∥*SO*[2]∥ *NO*[2] ), indicates AQI; *δ* denote pollutant concentration; *δ[L]* concentration breakpoint at (≤ δ); *δ[H]* concentration breakpoint at (≥δ); and *I[i]^H* , *I[i]^L* are index breakpoint to *δ[H]* and *δ[L]*, respectively.

## 3.4 Ensemble Learning (El)-Based Prediction

Instances of variables utilized for the prediction process include air quality parameters, weather data, and TIME (date/week/hour of ‘nth’ day), all of which may be found in the study. Data on air quality have been gathered from October 1, 2020, to May 31^(st), 2021. Graph neural network (GNN) and Random Forest are considered as base learners.

### 3.4.1 Graph Neural Network (Gnn)

This platform class uses graphs as test data and is known as a graph neural network (GNN). A class label is discovered by doing calculations on graphs. A single subjective component drives each recurrent unit in a GNN. Nonlinear recurrent modules receive inputs from both historical data and the results of current iterations.

The illustration in [Figure 3.2](#ch3.xhtml#fig3_2) depicts GCN’s general idea. The green node begins by averaging the mean values of its neighbors and its node ID. This value is then used to train a neural network on new information. Significantly, GCN makes use of a completely connected layer. Vectors in the second dimension tend to produce the desired results.

![Figure 3.2 Overall concept of GCN.](images/fig3_2_B.jpg)

[Figure 3.2 Overall concept of GCN.](#ch3.xhtml#R_fig3_2)

### 3.4.2 Random Forest

Using the bagging principle, another popular ML approach, random forest, mixes many decision trees to create a forest and randomization into the conceptual model. Individual trees are divided using a random set of features, while training sample subsets are created for every tree structure using a random distribution of samples. Once a node in a decision tree considers the optimum split for the randomized count of features. The algorithm will opt for the most prevalent result if the feature is categorical, whereas for numerical, the mean of total outcomes will be used.

Both regression and classification problems can be handled using random forest techniques. First, each testing data sample is run through the forest’s decision trees multiple times to enhance prediction. Trees are then used to make predictions by voting on a resultant. The prediction is then generated by a consensus majority between the trees, leading to an improved unique learner. As predicted, random forests are better than choice trees since the prediction means closer to the actual truth as a categorization (regression). [Figure 3.3](#ch3.xhtml#fig3_3) depicts a random forest made up of N random trees.

![Figure 3.3 Overall concept of random forest.](images/fig3_3_B.jpg)

[Figure 3.3 Overall concept of random forest.](#ch3.xhtml#R_fig3_3)

### 3.4.3 Ensemble Learning (El)

This study uses EL, which combines a variety of learning and optimization models to get better outcomes than any one of them alone could. These types of training and learning mostly use dependency injection and parallel processing. It suggests a high degree of reliance among a small group of datasets that sequentially create results, using strategies such as boosting.

EL’s core idea is to categorize or create results by using a variety of learning methodologies. Both voting and mean assessments may be used to combine the results of several classification algorithms. In linear or non-linear regression, data segmentation and mean assessment techniques are most often used, while voting methods are most commonly used in data labeling and classification. There are two sorts of voting: soft and hard. Classifiers vote independently, and a majority of those votes are authorized under the idea of “Hard voting,” also known as “qualified majority.”

Each classifier offers the probability estimates for one particular class label, one per data point; thus, soft voting may be regarded as a special kind of soft classifier. These probabilities are averaged out to determine which objective class label obtains the most votes. Many different ML based models may be combined into a single EL platform. It is possible that hybrid learning may improve the accuracy and resiliency of certain learning strategies, as well as overcome some of the limits of a particular approach. Basic learners, sometimes known as ‘weak learners,’ are among the incapacitated ones. ‘Strong learners’ are those who have achieved the baseline result and have built on it.

This section’s goal is to look at and contrast the three various approaches that were previously described. An EL strategy based on soft voting was proposed in this chapter. Class labels may be deduced using the classifier’s predicted probability p rather than the strict hard voting method. Baseline learners GCN and Random Forest are used in the initial phase of data processing. Once the accuracy of the baseline learners has been determined for each data point, the variances of those results are averaged. The EL forecast assessment is based on the soft voting results. [Figure 3.4](#ch3.xhtml#fig3_4) depicts the full process.

![Figure 3.4 Overall process of EL strategy.](images/fig3_4_B.jpg)

[Figure 3.4 Overall process of EL strategy.](#ch3.xhtml#R_fig3_4)

As a consequence of the observation period, moderate AQI levels are the most common from October to November. Conversely, unhealthy air quality is more common from December to April, showing that late winter and early spring are the most polluted seasons. From April to May, pollution levels generally reduce, with a modest spike in the early days of April. On average, more than 32.37% of instances fall into the moderate category, while 22.23% and 45.4% of cases go into the good and unhealthy categories, respectively.

Seasonal weather phenomena immobilize pollutants low to the land surface and keep them there longer in the winter, contributing to an increase in air pollution. As a result of pollution from industrial parks nearby as well as PM drifting in from neighboring states, the capital city has a reputation for having bad air quality.

[Figure 3.6](#ch3.xhtml#fig3_6) shows significant instances of the unhealthy class (dark red bars) air quality for the month of April and May, with reduced pollution levels only in October and November. The worst air quality is concentrated from December to May (more than 60% unhealthy). The higher AQI levels in New Delhi are also clustered in winter and spring, but October and November also featured significant instances of the unhealthy class (respectively 35%, 38%, and 41%).

##  3.6 Summary 

There seems to be a surge in pollution throughout the last decades, resulting in several ecological problems. The use of artificial intelligence (AI) algorithms for AQI prediction has shown encouraging outcomes. Furthermore, the fundamental objective of the Smart Emission Surveillance System is to make it more inventive, user-friendly, effective, and timesaving than the present system. A total of 8 months of data were received from the CCRAQM’s for training purposes, and a portion of the real-time dataset is constructed for testing and training purposes. The real-time datasets are perceived through the evolved smart air pollution monitoring system. Throughout the observation period, the quality of air in five parts of New Delhi (North, South, Central, West, and East) are considered for assessments due to their dire air quality conditions. The suggested method makes sensor outputs available via the internet for further inspection. With excellent outcomes for R², ensemble strategies provide the optimal target predictive accuracy based on the provided datasets. While the ensemble has better RMSE and accuracy, GCN has better MAE when used as a solo performer alongside the suggested one. This research also shows that the accuracy of the predictions differs by area across (India’s) country’s capital. The findings also show that when compared to other approaches, the EL techniques used in this work surpass them if examined. In this way, it may be seen as a distinct and better option for AQI prediction. In the future, we plan to extend the work on hybridizing the learning strategies with a large pool of datasets.
